Every persona is up unless the test passes its own `isPersonaUp`; a not-up case builds it with the real `createPersonaUpPredicate` rather than a hand-written check. `makePersonaClients(stubFor)` is the injected `clientFor`: each key gets its `makeStubSlack` stub's Web client, `setUnavailable(key)` makes the lookup return nothing (as before validation, in dry run or for an unknown key), and `calls` records every key asked for. `makeTrailCapture()` is the `emitTrail` sink. Read captured Slack calls with `posts(stub)`, `updates(stub)` and `slackCalls(...stubs)`. Script Slack failures on the stub (`post`, `update` and `open` queues). Each persona's client is a `RelaySlackClient` (`Pick<WebClient, 'chat' | 'conversations'>`), so a `dm` destination's `conversations.open` runs on the persona's own stub. Give each persona its own `makeStubSlack()`, so a call on the wrong client shows up as a non-empty `callLog` on another stub. To test a DM destination, give the persona `permission_prompts: 'dm'`, DMs on and a `dm.contact`. An unscripted open returns `stubOpenedDmId(contact)`; push `openedDm('D0X')` onto `script.open` to choose the ID, or `{ kind: 'platform', error: 'missing_scope' }` to fail the open. Assert the order with `stub.web.callLog.map((c) => c.method)` (`['conversations.open', 'chat.postMessage']`) and that the post names the `D…` ID, never the contact. The poller builds its own destination resolver unless `destinations` is passed; pass a `createPersonaDestinations` instance to share a DM cache with a notifier, and call `_resetPollerState()` in `beforeEach` so the default resolver's cache and default destination hold don't carry over. A case whose prompt or stuck-prompt warning can fail at the destination passes `destinationHold` built on a fake clock (see Destination Failure Tests). To test an update after a destination change, post the prompt first, then edit the applied persona (DMs off, or a new `permission_prompts`) and assert the update names the recorded conversation with no post or open.
- **JSONL safeguard effects**: `runJsonlPersistenceSafeguard` takes every effect through its `deps` (mountinfo, stat, AD `getRow`, `archiveCountSince`, `recordStartupError`, `home`), so it needs no `mock.module`. An `archiveCountSince` stub has the `ArchiveCountSince` shape `(channelIds, sinceEpochSeconds, ref) => number | null` and receives the persona's `delivery: all` channel IDs. Layer 2 skips a row whose `cwd` or `config_dir` label doesn't match the persona, so build rows with `cannedGetResult(overrides, persona, home)` and pass the same `home` as `deps.home`
- **Inbound delivery**: Test a delivery rule (subtypes, self-exclusion, DMs and group DMs, membership, mentions, `via`) in `tests/delivery-decision.test.ts` by calling the pure `decideDelivery` directly, and keep `tests/persona-routing.test.ts` for the pipeline's wiring of it. Test the pipeline through the real `src/persona-routing.ts`, never a copy of its logic, and build it with `makeRoutingHarness(specs, baseDir, opts?)` from `tests/test-helpers/persona-routing-harness.ts` instead of hand-wiring `createPersonaRouting` deps and registry fakes. The harness gives each persona a `makeStubSlack` stub (leak marker on), registers a session per persona in the real registry (fake transport and a server that records `notifications/claude/channel`), and installs restart fakes that record launches in `h.launches`. Options pick which personas have a session and which are `streamless`, `disconnected` or `throwOnNotify` (its `notification()` throws an error whose message carries `LEAK_SENTINEL` only inside a fake token and a `wss://…?ticket=` URL, so a log line that skips `redactSlackLogText` fails `assertNoLeak`); `onNotify` (per name) runs a hook when a session's `notification()` is called, awaited before it is recorded, to log the send, hold it open or fail it; `ackReaction` sets the `ack_reaction` the injected `getReplySettings` returns to both the routing and `h.reply` (none by default; chunking is always the default); `launchSession` replaces the launch outcome (hold it open to keep a persona in flight), and `restartDelayS` / `h.restartDelayS` set the restart delay. Every harness builds a new routing, so its per-persona dedupe stores start empty; `dedupeClock` injects their clock (pass a fake clock's `now` to test the retention window). `isPersonaUp` passes the routing an up predicate; without it every persona counts as up, so a not-up lost-message case (bug b.g57) passes one built with the real `createPersonaUpPredicate`. Feed events from the Slack stub's event factories with `h.receive(event, names?)`; it resolves once every persona's run has settled, so assert right after awaiting it. Read `h.p(name).stub.calls`, `h.p(name).notifications`, `h.allPosts()`, `h.logs` and `h.order`; `h.registerFor(name, opts)` adds or replaces a session mid-test, and `h.captured()` feeds `assertNoLeak`. `h.reply(name, args)` makes persona `name`'s `reply` tool call through the real session server with the same clients, the real ack tracker and the same reply settings as the routing, closes its own MCP client, and rejects when the tool reports an error. Use it to observe an ack entry the routing recorded, as a `reactions.remove` on the persona's own stub; don't wire a separate reply path. The routing's `notify` is the real `createPersonaNotifier` (`h.notifier`) over the same stubs, so a lost-message notice is a `chat.postMessage` on the persona's own stub at its destination (for a `dm` destination, after `conversations.open`). The notifier is built by `makeNotifierStack` and is never in dry run (test dry run through `makeNotifierHarness`); its destination hold (`h.hold`) runs on the harness's own fake clock (`h.holdClock`), and its lines land in `h.logs`. `h.notices` records every notice the routing raised (key and body, no persona prefix); `h.noticeText(name, body)` is the text the notifier posts; `h.postsTo(channel)` is every post to one conversation on any stub, so assert "nothing in the source" as `expect(h.postsTo(source)).toEqual([])`. To tell which recovery state a notice reports, use `stateOf(text)` from the same file (the one state its `LOST_STATE_PHRASES` wording identifies, or `none` / `several`) and loop over `LOST_MESSAGE_STATES` (all five, `not-up` first); don't write a second copy of the state phrases. A notice raised while `h.clients.setUnavailable(key)` is held until `h.notifier.flush(key)`; the `notify` option replaces the notifier (for example with a throwing sink), still recorded in `h.notices`. The default `resolveUserName` is `users.info` on the persona's client from `h.clients.clientFor(key)`, falling back to the user ID when the persona has no client, as `server.ts` does. The Slack stub's `users.info` can't be scripted to fail, so pass a rejecting `resolveUserName` to test a failed sender lookup. A case that can hold a notice calls `h.hold.cancelAll()` in `afterEach`. Call `resetRoutingState()` in `beforeEach` and `waitFor(cond)` for the fast restart timer. Put `baseDir` in a temp directory, never the real home (b.av2 SR-13.2). `tests/persona-routing.test.ts` is the reference
- **Importing server.ts**: A test may import `src/server.ts`'s exports directly (for example `isHttpVerbose`, the keep-alive helpers, `_buildIsSessionAliveAdapter`). Importing it reads no token, builds no Slack client and creates no directory; at import it only computes its state paths from `SLACK_STATE_DIR` or the home, and registers its SIGTERM/SIGINT handlers. Because the paths are computed at import, the SR-13.2 isolation rules still apply:
  - No test calls `main()` or runs `server.ts` as an entry point in-process.
  - No test sets HOME or `SLACK_STATE_DIR` process-wide in order to import `server.ts`.
  - A check that must exercise `server.ts`'s import or start behaviour against a HOME runs it in a child `bun` process with a `mkdtempSync` HOME and `SLACK_STATE_DIR` and a host-safe environment (`runInFakeHome` from `tests/test-helpers/fake-home-subprocess.ts`), as `tests/server-startup-wiring.test.ts` does.

  Put logic under test in an importable module and test it there, as inbound delivery, the start bring-up (`src/persona-start.ts`) and the event router do. Replicate server.ts logic in a self-contained test server only for HTTP handling that still lives inside `main()` (see Self-Contained Test Servers)
- **server.ts wiring and call order**: To prove a call is wired into `main()` exactly once, or runs before another, audit the source text instead of importing it. Read `src/server.ts` with `readFileSync`, pass it through `stripComments` from `tests/test-helpers/source-audit.ts`, and compare offsets from `indicesOf(regex, code)` (the regex must be global). To pin a call's arguments, use the same file's parsers rather than a hand-rolled regex: `callsOf` and `onlyCallArguments` (throws unless there is exactly one call), `callArguments` and `balancedAfter` for bracketed text, `splitTopLevel` and `objectProperties` to read an argument list or object literal. To anchor on the start, use `startResolution(code)`: it returns the reload controller's and the start outcome's names, the applied-config variable (`loaded`) and the offsets of `createReloadController(`, `resolveStart(`, the `<loaded> = <outcome>.config` assignment and `runStartBringUp(`, and throws unless each is the only one of its kind. It only locates the start: what else assigns the applied config is checked by the refused-start test in `tests/server-startup-wiring.test.ts` alone, so don't repeat that check elsewhere. `loadedConfigName(code)` is its `loaded`. `mainBody(code)` gives the `[start, end)` of `main()`'s body and `insideMain(code, offset)` says whether an offset lies in it; `shutdownBody(code)` gives the same span for `async function shutdown(`, so a check that a call runs on the shutdown path uses it rather than locating `shutdown` by hand, as `tests/reload-wiring.test.ts`, `tests/cron-scheduler-wiring.test.ts`, `tests/permission-relay-wiring.test.ts` and `tests/server-startup-wiring.test.ts` do. A pass that must run over the applied config comes after `assignAt`; one that must precede the per-persona bring-up comes before `bringUpAt`, not before the `startupSessionManager` text, which now sits in the controller's construction. Always audit the stripped text: a comment or commented-out call that names the function would otherwise satisfy or skew the assertion. `tests/start-sweep-wiring.test.ts`, `tests/jsonl-safeguard-wiring.test.ts` and `tests/server-startup-wiring.test.ts` are the references. Anchor an audit on content that stays in `main()` (a call name, its arguments), never on line numbers or on code that has moved to a module: the Slack handlers now live in the event router, so what a connection does with an event is tested by calling `createPersonaEventRouter`, not by auditing `server.ts`. Audit only what cannot be driven by a behaviour test (a call's presence, its arguments, its order in `main()`)

### Module Mocks (mock.module)

**The hazard.** `bun test` runs every test file in a single process. `mock.module(...)` patches the module registry at the process level — it is process-global state. A top-level `mock.module(...)` call fires at import time and is never cleaned up, so the patched module leaks into every subsequent test file that imports the same module. If the mock factory omits an export that a later file imports, the later file crashes at runtime.

**The b.b8s incident.** A top-level mock in one file replaced `child_process` with only `{ spawn: fakeSpawn }`. A later file did `import { spawnSync } from 'child_process'` and failed with:

```text
SyntaxError: Export named 'spawnSync' not found in module 'node:child_process'
```

The root cause and class-of-bug fix are tracked in bugs b.b8s and b.5wd.

**The rule.** Every `mock.module(...)` call must live inside a `beforeEach` or `beforeAll` block, paired with a matching `afterEach` or `afterAll` that calls `mock.restore()`. Never call `mock.module(...)` or `mock.restore()` at file top-level. This includes calls inside a `describe(...)` body but outside hooks — those run at import/registration time and leak the same way.

**Don't:**

```typescript
// BAD — top-level, leaks into all subsequent test files
mock.module('child_process', () => ({ spawn: fakeSpawn }))

describe('MyTest', () => { ... })
```

**Do:**

```typescript
import { mock } from 'bun:test'

describe('MyTest', () => {
  beforeEach(async () => {
    // Spread the real module so later imports get all original exports unchanged
    const real = await import('node:child_process')
    mock.module('node:child_process', () => ({ ...real, spawn: fakeSpawn }))
  })

  afterEach(() => {
    mock.restore()
  })

  test('uses fakeSpawn', () => { ... })
})
```

**Fake children must behave like real ones.** Under Bun 1.4, `mock.restore()` does not undo `mock.module`, so a `child_process` fake stays installed for later files. A fake `spawn` must return an EventEmitter-like child (`on`/`once`, `stdout`/`stderr` streams, `unref`, `kill`, `pid`) that emits `exit` and `close`. Prefer injection over a module mock: the CLI's daemon spawn is a `CliDeps` seam (`spawnDaemon`), so CLI tests pass a fake child there and need no `child_process` mock.

**Put the real exports back yourself.** Because `mock.restore()` leaves the module mocked, a file that mocks a package copies its real namespace at import (`const REAL = { ...ns }` from `import * as ns`) and, in `afterEach`, calls `mock.module(<name>, () => REAL)` before `mock.restore()`. Build each fake over the copy (`{ ...REAL, fn: fake }`) so every other export stays real, and to prove the real exports are back, compare the live namespace with the copy entry by entry in `afterAll`, never naming an export. For `agent-director`, prefer an injected seam: no test mocks the package, and the install check and the postinstall probe take their resolver and install check as injected dependencies. Spreading the namespace or its copy into a `mock.module` factory is allowed although it carries `Client` and `resolveSystemBinary`; reading either from it is not (see Host safety under Isolation).

**Enforcement.** `scripts/check-no-toplevel-mock-module.ts` runs as part of `pretest`. It scans all files under `tests/` and fails the test run if any line at column 0 (zero leading whitespace) contains `mock.module(` or `mock.restore(`.

### Throwaway Git Repos

Fixtures that create a throwaway git repo and commit in it use one canonical setup. Reference implementation: `initRepo` in `tests/publish-promote-bun-g-cwd.test.ts`. `tests/audit-finished-tickets-script.test.ts` follows it (temp HOME, `GIT_CONFIG_NOSYSTEM=1` on every child that runs git, the repo-local fake identity and `commit.gpgsign false`, one commit) with one difference: its HOME is a separate `audit-home-*` `mkdtempSync` directory outside the fixture project, so the audit script can't mistake it for a hive.

- **git runs under a temp HOME.** Every git child, and every script child that runs git, gets its `env` from a direct `hostSafeChildEnv(fixtureHome, { tools: ['git', …], extras: { GIT_CONFIG_NOSYSTEM: '1' } })` call. In the reference file the fixture HOME is an empty directory inside the fixture's `mkdtempSync` root, next to the repo, removed with it; a fixture whose script scans the tree around the repo (as the audit script does) puts HOME in its own temp directory instead. The temp HOME keeps the host's global git configuration out and `GIT_CONFIG_NOSYSTEM=1` keeps the system one out, so no host identity, hooks path, signing key or credential reaches git.
- **The repo declares its own fake identity.** With no global configuration there is no identity, so the fixture sets a repo-local `user.name` and `user.email` (the reference file's constants: `Fixture`, `test@example.invalid`; never a real person or domain) and `commit.gpgsign false`.
- **Commit once.** Nothing on the host can reject or rewrite the commit, so it succeeds on the first attempt on any host. Don't wrap it in a retry.

```typescript
git(fx, 'init', '-q')
git(fx, 'config', 'user.name', FIXTURE_USER_NAME)
git(fx, 'config', 'user.email', FIXTURE_USER_EMAIL)
git(fx, 'config', 'commit.gpgsign', 'false')
...
git(fx, 'commit', '-q', '-m', 'init')
```

The reference file has a case asserting that the fixture commit's author is the repo-local fake identity, which fails if anything rewrote or overrode it. It does not prove that no host git configuration reached git: a repo-local `user.*` value overrides a global or system one, so the identity would match either way.

**Do not isolate git by other means.** No `core.hooksPath` redirects, no `--no-verify`, no `GIT_AUTHOR_*`/`GIT_COMMITTER_*` overrides, and no env built outside `hostSafeChildEnv`.

### Self-Contained Test Servers

Test an HTTP endpoint through its real handler module, not a copy of its logic. `/interject` is the reference: `tests/interject.test.ts` imports `handleInterject` from `src/interject.ts` and injects its dependencies (a persona config from `makeMultiPersonaConfig`, stub sessions keyed by persona key, a fixed clock, a capturing logger). When a case needs a real HTTP request, (the 32 KB cap and a loopback delivery), it serves the real handler from a port-0 `Bun.serve()`. When logic still lives in `server.ts`, move it into an importable module. Its exported helpers can be imported directly (see Importing server.ts above), but the `/mcp` fetch handler is built inside `main()` with `Bun.serve`, which no test may run, so a stand-in server remains the way to test that branch until it moves (`sse-timeout.test.ts` mirrors the MCP GET/POST branch this way). Prove `server.ts` delegates to the handler with a source-text audit (see server.ts wiring above).

A stand-in `/interject` server is still the right tool when the code under test is an HTTP client of the endpoint: `cron-dispatch.test.ts` records the dispatcher's POSTs and scripts its responses. `cron-scheduler-interject.test.ts` instead serves the real `handleInterject` on port 0, so a scheduler fire is checked end to end through the real handler. For any `Bun.serve()` in a test:

- Bind to port 0 (random available port) to avoid conflicts
- Share Maps between test code and server handler via closure
- Stop the server in afterAll()

### Reading Shipped Markdown

Tests that read the README or a skill use the shared helper `tests/test-helpers/markdown.ts`, not a file-local heading scanner. It is fence-aware: a `# comment` in a shell block is never a heading, and a section ends at the next heading of the same or a higher level.

- `headings(text)`: every heading outside fenced code, with its line, level, title and `## Title` form.
- `sectionRange(text, match)` and `findSection(text, match)`: the line range or the body of the first matching section, undefined when none matches. `match` is the heading as written (`'## Title'`, exact) or a RegExp over that form, never with the `g` or `y` flag.
- `requiredSection(text, match, file)`: `findSection` that throws `<file> has no heading …`, so a moved or renamed section fails naming it instead of a check passing on an empty string. Prefer it to `findSection`.
- `splitFences(text)`: the prose outside fenced blocks, and each block's info word and exact body. Take a shipped command or example from its section's one block and fail on any other count or tag, as `tests/credentials-command.test.ts` does.
- `headingSlug(title)` and `headingAnchors(text)`: GitHub's anchor for a title, and every heading's anchor in order (a repeat gets `-1`, `-2`), for checking that a link's `#anchor` resolves.
- `flat(text)`: whitespace collapsed, so a phrase matches across a line wrap.
- `classHeading(label)`: a RegExp for the `###` heading naming a class label as a code span, with any title after it; the label is matched literally and a longer label does not match. Pass it to `requiredSection` to read a class's entry.

Find a section by its title, never by its step number (a whole-word RegExp such as `/^### .*\bAdd a persona$/`), so renumbering a step doesn't break the test. `tests/markdown-helper.test.ts` covers the helper.

### Shipped-Description Audit (AC 46)

`tests/shipped-docs.test.ts` is the one audit of what the package ships to operators and bot instances: the whole README, every file under `skills/`, the whole manifest, `MCP_INSTRUCTIONS`, the Slack Reply Guard's reminder text and `CRONTABLE_TEMPLATE_HEADER` (see its row under Test File Organization). The MCP tool descriptions are not audited, since the tool list isn't exported. `CHANGELOG.md` and `docs/` are not shipped descriptions and are not scanned.

- **One term list.** A retired term goes in `FORBIDDEN_TERMS`, in the group it belongs to with its SR cited, and gets a sample in `SAMPLES` (the self-check fails until it has one). Don't write a second forbidden-term check in a skill's own test; the wizard's shell-token checks were moved into the audit for that reason.
- **One exemption.** `AUDIT_EXCEPTIONS` holds only the debugging skill's `## Pre-persona configuration` entry, for the three pre-persona keys. Fix a real hit by rewriting the shipped text in persona terms; never add an exemption, an allow-list entry or a narrower pattern to make it pass.
- **Wording that must stay legal.** If a term would catch legitimate persona wording, narrow its pattern and pin the wording both ways: a "not flagged" self-check on in-memory text, and a `NOT_HITS_BY_DESIGN` entry tied to the file and the reason when the wording is in shipped text.
- **Instructions through the export.** Read the MCP instruction text as the exported `MCP_INSTRUCTIONS`, never from `src/registry.ts`'s source text.
- **No persona-name rule.** Shipped text describes no persona-name format (b.av2 SR-1.2), so no case expects one.
- **Mutation checks off the tree.** Prove a case fails by editing an in-memory or `mkdtempSync` copy; a shipped file edited in place to probe a case is restored byte for byte.

## Assertions

- Use `expect(x).toBe(y)` for primitives
- Use `expect(x).toEqual(y)` for objects/arrays
- Use `expect(x).toBeUndefined()` / `toBeDefined()` for presence checks
- Avoid `.toBeTruthy()` / `.toBeFalsy()` — be specific about expected values
- When testing async behavior through closures, use non-null assertions (`resolved!`) if TypeScript narrows incorrectly

## What to Test

- Happy path for each public function
- Error cases (invalid input, missing data, API failures)
- Edge cases from the SRD/PRD
- Concurrent operations (e.g., multiple pending requests)
- Cleanup on abort/disconnect
- State isolation between tests (no leaking via module-scoped Maps)

## What NOT to Test

- Internal implementation details (private helper functions)
- Exact log output (test behavior, not logging)
- Timing-dependent behavior with real delays — inject the clock and timers (see Backoff and Retry Tests); older suites use short configurable timeouts
- Test infrastructure itself (factories, stubs, reset helpers) — if a fixture breaks, the real tests that use it will fail anyway

## Keeping the Suite Lean

The goal is good coverage with as few lines of test code as possible. Apply the 80/20 rule: strong coverage of behavior does not require testing every input permutation — don't gold-plate.

- Test behavior, not implementation: assert on what a function produces (return values, state changes, captured calls), not on how it got there. A test needing 3+ stubs to run is usually testing implementation details and will break on harmless refactors.
- Prefer removing a redundant test over keeping it "just in case"
- Prefer `test.each` over copy-pasted test functions
- Prefer factory functions over repeated inline setup blocks
